<!--
Hi everyone! I’m Josh – I’m a full time independent open source maintainer. I focus on static analysis tooling around JavaScript & TypeScript – most notably, typescript-eslint, the tooling that allows you to run ESLint and Prettier on TypeScript code.
I’m also the author of the recent Learning TypeScript book, published by O’Reilly.
-->

<!--
This is the technical, precise term for TypeScript: that it’s a “superset” of JavaScript. Meaning, it’s a programming language that contains all the syntax of JavaScript, plus new syntax.
-->

<!--
JavaScript is a programming language. It provides a standard set of syntax to describe values and how to use those values: variables, string literals, assignments, and so on.
The big problem with JavaScript is that it doesn’t have a built-in, standardized way to describe the intent behind those values. This code snippet shows creating a value variable, then assigning it first a string, then a number. There’s no way in JavaScript to say, hey, I want this value to hold a string.
-->

---

hide: true
transition: { name: view-transition, duration: 500 }
---

<!--
The TypeScript programming language looks a lot like
TypeScript adds in some new syntax to the world of JavaScript. Here we see a ‘type annotation’ indicating that our value variable should only ever be a string.
-->

<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:138.14px;left:46.91px;top:294.33px;width:866.18px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px;line-height:32.4px"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">$ </span><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">tsc </span><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">index.ts</span><br><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">index.ts</span><span style="color:#00B0F0;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">:</span><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">3</span><span style="color:#FFFF00;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">:</span><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">1</span><span style="color:#FFFF00;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px"> - </span><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">error </span><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">TS2322</span><span style="color:#4472C4;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">: Type 'number' is not assignable to type 'string'.</span></p><p style="font-size:18px;line-height:21.6px"><br></p><p style="font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px;line-height:21.6px"><span style="background-color:#C8D7FA;color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">3</span><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px"> value = 1337;</span></p><p style="font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px;line-height:21.6px"><span style="background-color:#C8D7FA;color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">  </span><span style="color:#FF0000;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">~~~~~</span></p></div>

<!--
There is also a program called the TypeScript type checker that can run on your code and tell you if there are any mismatches between the way code constructs such as variables were initialized and how they’re used.
Here, we see the TypeScript command-line, ”tsc”, telling us that an index.ts file mismatches in its third line of code. We said that our value variable is a string, but then gave it a number. What gives?!
-->

---

layout: none
hide: true
transition: { name: fade, duration: 350 }
---

<!--
Lastly, TypeScript also provides nice integrations for editors such as VS Code, called language services. Editors can continuously run TypeScript’s language services to help you during development. If you’re already a TypeScript developer you’re probably intimately familiar with the “red squiggly”. Red squigglies are provided by language services to tell you that, if you hover your mouse over the squigglied code, you should read the TypeScript complaint for it.
-->

<!--
This variable, ‘value’, is given an initial value of type string. That means value is only ever allowed to be a string. If TypeScript sees that we try to provide it a number, it’ll yell at us in the form of a type error.
Note that this code is syntactically valid – so it can be transpiled down to output JavaScript. But TypeScript will log an error when doing so.
But, what if we have a variable whose type can’t be inferred rom the initial value? In that case, we’ll need some way of indicating to TypeScript what the intended type is meant to be.
https://www.typescriptlang.org/play/?#code/DYUwLgBAbghsCuIIF4ICIBKIYGMxoG4AoIgSwDMIAKAWRjAAsA6AJxgDsATAewFsqAlBAB8EAAxMArEIDeRCAuhxEKdAGV4vXqXzEAvhBDAAzkjmKlCJKgCMAZjsB2YhYD0riABUAngAckAOTsmgBGICwBEKTGEOzckDDGxqQA5uwwIaAQYNzZfoHGYCyk7CkBTER6REA

<!--
Enter type annotations: a way to indicate to TypeScript the type of a construct such as a variable.
Variables that are given a type annotation also stay the same type always. Here we’re saying that value is type string, and so are not allowed to provide a number (or any other not-string typed values).
Keep in mind that type annotations are purely a development-time construct. They’re erased when your TypeScript syntax is transpiled over to JavaScript.
https://www.typescriptlang.org/play/?#code/DYUwLgBAbghsCuIBcEDOYBOBLAdgcwG4AoIrAMwgAoBZGMACwDoMYcATAewFtKBKCAHwQADIwCs-AN5EIs6HEQQAvBABEAZXhcuWMKuIBfCCGCoQEaXPkJzKgIwBmBwHZiVgPTuIAFQCeAB3MAchwtACMQDCCILFQIHA5IGFRULDwcGDDQCDAOHIDg9Gx8IMYiAyIgA
-->

<!--
If we don’t provide an initial value for our variables, TypeScript will treat it as what’s called an evolving any. Meaning, the variable’s type will start out as any – it can be anything. Which is super unsafe! We don’t like the any type! It allows us to bypass all sorts of type checking.
But, whenever we assign a value to the variable, TypeScript will narrow the variable’s type to that value’s type. It ‘evolves’ the variable’s type as it’s used.
https://www.typescriptlang.org/play/?#code/DYUwLgBAbghsCuIDcED0qIBUCeAHEAXBDAHbYBQ5sCIEAvBAEQAiMATgJYyMrpZ6EIAZzCcSAcypxEAOjAB7AKq58bAMIwhIABQBKXhgDyAa0rVE9CAEYAzDYDsB-viIl4AWwBGINlJpz5AAU2EABjDiEOeRJtK300I1M-WQVlVQ0tPSRyPgBRNjZ5NiIAclSVHwyQEogAE3kQIQgSeUgQAA8IyGiIMAEIErcvHxKZciA
-->

<!--
If you’d like to describe a union type, you can do so in a type annotation just like any other type. The | ”pipe” operator, commonly pronounced “or”, indicates it’s a union type. A union type is one where the value can be one of several possible types, or “constituents”. This is a not-too-uncommon JavaScript pattern, because JavaScript doesn’t stop you from changing the shape of a variable after it’s created. Therefore, TypeScript must support this.
In this example, the ‘value’ variable is of a union type where the constituents are string and number.
Assigning values that are assignable to any of the constituents in the union type is allowed. But types that aren’t one of the constituents, like the boolean in the example, aren’t.
https://www.typescriptlang.org/play/?#code/DYUwLgBAbghsCuIBcEDOYBOBLAdgcwgB8Id4BbAIxAwG4AoO2BECAXggCIA1agexywBrGB3pNEbCAEYAzDIDs9RnAntMiegHpNEACoBPAA4sA5BV69QMHCYhZUJXpBipUWPDhgVQEML19GpujY+EQk5FQYJgB0dEA
-->

<!--
Union types can also be inferred from the initial value of a variable. TypeScript can see that this ‘value’ variable is only ever a string (some of the time) or a number (some of the time). That lets it be inferred as the union of string or number.

<!--
A natural next question might be, if TypeScript is so strict about property accesses of union types… how can we use them? If I have some code that only runs when a union typed value is known to be one of those type constituents, can we then access more properties on the value?
The answer is: yes! TypeScript understands a concept called “narrowing”. If some area of your code only happens when a particular value is a particular type, then in that area of code, we can narrow down that value’s type.
In this example, the ‘if’ statement’s body can only be executed if the value is a string. So inside the if statement, TypeScript can narrow down the value to be just type string. Accessing its ‘.toUpperCase()’ is allowed.
https://www.typescriptlang.org/play/?#code/MYewdgzgLgBAbgQwDYFcCmMC8MCyCoAWAdAE4JgAmIAtgBQCUMAfDAAxECsMA-DAEQAZNGT4AoGBJgAuGAEYAzPIDsAblGjEqNESggAqgAcDwgMIIIaBmtEB6GzAAKJEMZJQAnjADkuw67MWXjBUaBAwYCCwaAAeAJbQMOAwHsbegsIIfDAAPnKKSl5EorEAZjC0KWggZZroWJjYfNAksWAA5nyMAN7ikrXavkam5pb0agC+okA
-->

const literal = <Value extends string>(value: Value): SchemaLiteral<Value> => ({
value,
zType: "literal",
}); // Unions
interface SchemaUnion<Values extends any[]> {
values: Values;
zType: "union";
}
const union = <Values extends any[]>(values: Values): SchemaUnion<Values> => ({
values,
zType: "union",
});
type UnwrapSchemaUnion<Values> = Values extends (infer Value)[]
? Infer<Value>
: never;
type Infer<T> =
// Primitives (string)
T extends SchemaString
? string
: // Literals
T extends SchemaLiteral<infer Value>
? Value
: // Unions
T extends SchemaUnion<infer Values>
? UnwrapSchemaUnion<Values>
: never;
const plan = union([literal("active"), literal("improvising")]);
type Plan = Infer<typeof plan>;

<!--
// Primitives
interface SchemaString {
primitive: "string";
zType: "primitive";
}
const string = (): SchemaString => ({
primitive: "string",
zType: "primitive",
}); // Literals
interface SchemaLiteral<Value> {
value: Value;
zType: "literal";
}
const literal = <Value extends string>(value: Value): SchemaLiteral<Value> => ({
value,
zType: "literal",
}); // Unions
interface SchemaUnion<Values extends any[]> {
values: Values;
zType: "union";
}
const union = <Values extends any[]>(values: Values): SchemaUnion<Values> => ({
values,
zType: "union",
});
type UnwrapSchemaUnion<Values> = Values extends (infer Value)[]
? Infer<Value>
: never; // Objects
interface SchemaObject<Properties> {
properties: Properties;
zType: "object";
}
const object = <Properties>(
properties: Properties
): SchemaObject<Properties> => ({
properties,
zType: "object",
});
type UnwrapSchemaObject<Properties> = {
[K in keyof Properties]: Infer<Properties[K]>;
};
type Infer<T> =
// Primitives (string)
T extends SchemaString
? string
: // Literals
T extends SchemaLiteral<infer Value>
? Value
: // Unions
T extends SchemaUnion<infer Values>
? UnwrapSchemaUnion<Values>
: // Objects
T extends SchemaObject<infer Properties>
? UnwrapSchemaObject<Properties>
: never;
const spySchema = object({
disguise: string(),
moniker: literal("007"),
plan: union([literal("active"), literal("improvising")]),
});
type Spy = Infer<typeof spySchema>;
-->
